George Douglas Papers annotated transcription

Version 1.1 · September 2026 · 387 source scan pages

AI-assisted contribution for repository review. Unreadable and tentative passages remain marked. Open the unresolved-readings register.

Editorial guide

Purpose and scope

This edition offers a direct, annotated transcription of the six supplied PDFs of the George Douglas Papers, 1830–1939, Center for Restoration Studies MS 5, Abilene Christian University Special Collections and Archives. It accounts for all 387 scan pages, including repository covers, later identifying slips, envelopes, duplicate images and fragments. Some damaged passages remain only partly transcribed. Folders 4–7 described in the finding aid were not supplied as manuscript scans and are outside this edition.

Preparation and review

Prepared for Volney Douglas with AI assistance in September 2026, from visual reading of the supplied scans. Each scan received an initial reading. Version 1.1 adds local OCR processing of all 193 pages containing the original 1,200 uncertainties, and applies selected image-checked corrections. OCR completion is not a complete new visual check; the item ledger records which passages were individually adjudicated. The page inventory identifies the review level. This edition has not received an independent human transcription check or repository approval. It is offered for scholarly review and correction, and does not replace the source images.

Independence

The first edition was prepared separately from the plain-English family edition. For version 1.1, the other agent’s contextual review and the family edition were consulted to guide image checks; this revision is not a blinded comparison. Part of a pre-existing 25-page transcription was viewed during the initial inventory, before the request for independence; it has not been used for this transcription. The work is therefore not a blind experiment.

Wording and layout

Original wording, spelling, grammar, abbreviations and recoverable punctuation are retained. No silent expansion or modernization is intended. Handwritten capitalization, punctuation and word division sometimes require judgment. Superscripts are lowered to the baseline. Ordinary line wrapping is reflowed; verse lines, addresses and separate text blocks are distinguished. Underlining and decorative features are not systematically encoded. This is a direct reading transcript, not a facsimile or a fully diplomatic edition.

Uncertainty notation

Double square brackets contain editorial intervention. [[illegible]] means writing could not be read; an unspecified gap does not imply a word count. Adjacent unreadable spans may be combined. A stated extent, such as [[illegible: approximately 3 words]], is an estimate. [[word?]] gives a tentative reading, including tentative phrases. [[lost: torn edge]] records writing missing at a damaged edge; [[obscured: fold]] identifies an obstruction. Original single brackets remain as written. No missing wording is supplied solely to produce a fluent sentence.

Corrections and separate blocks

[[deleted: text]] records readable canceled text; [[deleted: illegible]] records an unread cancellation. [[inserted: text]] records a significant insertion in its intended position. [[margin]] and [[/margin]] enclose marginal writing. [[postmark]] and [[postage]] identify postal text. [[blank]], [[seal]] and [[sketch]] identify useful non-textual features. A [[separate block: description]] marker introduces an independently positioned passage. Handwriting or cancellation may itself be uncertain.

Source order and later descriptions

Entries follow the PDFs’ scan order. Where the leaves appear out of sequence, the editorial note suggests a reading order without moving the entry. Later identifying slips are transcribed separately; their names, dates and attributions are not silently substituted for manuscript readings. Duplicate scans retain separate entries. Apparent contradictions are recorded rather than reconciled.

Documentary statements

The letters preserve their writers’ assertions, beliefs, rumors and opinions. Transcription does not establish those statements as historical fact. Historical racial language and other offensive wording remain where legible. The original scans are the evidence against which this typescript should be checked.

References and corrections

A page ID such as 05-025 means source 05, PDF scan page 25, counting the cover as page 1. A block ID such as 05-025-P02 adds the second transcript paragraph or block. These are editorial locators; they are not manuscript page numbers. Suggested citation: George Douglas Papers annotated transcription, version 1.1, 05-025-P02; corresponding source 05, scan page 25. For a correction, give that locator, the existing wording, a proposed reading, and the image or other evidence supporting it.

Files for reuse

The package includes four searchable PDFs and four editable Word files, a complete UTF-8 transcript, structured JSON, an offline HTML reading copy, a page inventory, and an unresolved-readings register in TSV and HTML. The register retains the original uncertainty IDs; resolved IDs are retired and new IDs use higher numbers. The review ledger preserves all 1,200 baseline items. The register includes tentative readings, unread text, loss and obstruction; structural markers and readable insertions or deletions are not unresolved items. Original source PDFs are identified by filename and SHA-256 checksum but are not duplicated in this package.

Repository credit

Original documents and scans: George Douglas Papers, 1830–1939, Center for Restoration Studies MS 5, Abilene Christian University Special Collections and Archives, Brown Library, Abilene Christian University, Abilene, Texas. Collection address printed in the supplied files: https://digitalcommons.acu.edu/george_douglas. This contribution acknowledges the repository’s work in preserving and scanning the collection. No repository endorsement or transfer of copyright is asserted.

Transcription guidance

The general approach of retaining original spelling and abbreviations and marking uncertainty is informed by the Smithsonian Transcription Center’s How to Transcribe guidance, consulted September 2026: https://transcription.si.edu/instructions/transcribe. This edition defines its own notation and is not a Smithsonian project.

02-003 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 02 - L. Douglas Sermon Notes, 16 Feb 1883.pdf · Scan page 3

02-003-P01Of all the thoughts enclosed in the language of Inspiration [[lost: left edge]] its laws its precepts its promises what is there that conveys more, to our minds and hearts than is expressed in this short sentence? Jesus wept. Let us look a moment at the character of Jesus: who, and what is he? If we accept the truths of the Bible, he is the son of God, The King of the Jews, The bright and morning star! A ruler of the Jews came to Jesus and said, "we know thou art a teacher come from God for no man can do the miracles that thou doest, except God be with him." Look then at the power of this man: by his word water is turned into wine. The blind receive their sight, the lame walk. By His word the tempest is stilled, and He walks the waves of the wild Galilee. By His word the dead are called back to life again. Truly He was the son of God.
02-003-P02But it is not from the Bible alone that we have testimony in regard to the greatness of this man. Renan in his Life of Christ says "All ages will proclaim that among the sons of men there were none born greater than Jesus." His goodness, [[greatness?]], his omnipotent power, we cannot doubt. We stand in awe as we contemplate them, and our hearts acknowledge him as King of Kings and Lord of Lords. But while we contemplate all of this glorious majesty our eye falls upon this simple sentence Jesus wept. What do we learn by this? by these two short words. We learn that He had a heart that could be touched by the sorrows and Suffering of our frail humanity: that Sympath[[lost: torn edge]]

Editorial note First word and left edge of the second line are damaged. The terminal word is torn; it has not been completed from context. Text continues on scan page 4.

02-004 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 02 - L. Douglas Sermon Notes, 16 Feb 1883.pdf · Scan page 4

02-004-P01the power to "make e'en the wretched blessed" had its abode in His breast and that He could be touched by a feeling of our infirmities. How our hearts thrill with emotions of Love and thankfulness when we think of this blessed truth, that He was in sympathy with the sorrows of frail humanity.
02-004-P02More than eighteen hundred years have been measured by the revolving Earth since Jesus walked the shores and waves of Galilee, but the influence of His words and life, are still felt. And down through the vista of the coming Ages they will be felt till time shall be no more. And with all the records of His wonderful life this simple truth will descend with the others, Jesus wept.
02-004-P03L. Douglas. Sylvana Feb 16th 1883
02-004-P04Forever reign victorious King Wide through the earth thy name be known; And call our longing Souls to sing, Sublimest anthems near thy throne.

Editorial note Continues the damaged sentence at the end of scan page 3. The final four lines are verse.

03-003 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 03 - Sylvana Church Endorsement for George Douglas, Sept 1888.pdf · Scan page 3

03-003-P01This certifies that Bro. George Douglas, who is a member of the Church of Christ worshiping at Sylvana Ellis County Texas, has for some length of time been laboring in word and doctrine, and wishing him to continue in the work, we take pleasure in stating that he goes with our full permission & hearty approval. May he realize with the Apostle Paul. "Woe is me if I preach not the Gospel of Christ" - Ever remembering the Solemn Charge to Timothy to preach the Word &c -

Editorial note Several letters fade at the right edge. Hyphenation in Gospel at the line break is joined. The final abbreviation is read as &c.

04-004 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 4

04-004-P01Brushy Ellis County Texas Friday Jan 29th 1864
04-004-P02Mr George Douglas Dear [[Bro?]] & Cousin,
04-004-P03By the above Caption You are informed of my Whereabouts After an absence of twenty odd months I arrived at [[illegible]] on 22d of the month Since which time I have mingled with the friends of the [[illegible]]. Truly I have had a pleasant time - The courteous manner in which I have been every where received and the kindness shown to me has been truly gratifying to me personally. I have just returned from Aunt Betties and concluded I would chat you a little while through the medium as a substitute, to an oral one.
04-004-P04I wrote to you last Sept but have never learned the destination of my letter - [[deleted: illegible]] you did not return it, [[deleted: illegible]] if you did I did not reply [[illegible]] this one will not prove offensive to you. Since Feb 1st - I have been on detached Service the greater portion of the time - Last Feb was sent to the Ouachita river Country to gather forage for the Command. Was engaged in this business two months. [[illegible]] of my [[Solitariness?]] passed more pleasantly the first month excepted than [[illegible]] to you all "bibs", and as a matter of course, [[illegible]] pleased more than the ladies in the prettiest Looks - Smiles & kindest [[illegible]] and when the pot boiled the strongest and being not entirely [[illegible]] of the influence of the gentle fair, on some occasions I [[illegible]] their claims. And was almost persuaded (I like to have said taken unawares) [[illegible]] the "equal" in [[gentility?]] & [[illegible]] of the Young ladies of my former [[illegible]] - The brindle of my acquaintances [[illegible]] shirts [[illegible]] as they seem to have especial claims of Superiority [[illegible]].
04-004-P05But Enough on this subject as it is hardly entitled to a place in these times of strife & Confusion - Whilst the all absorbing all engrossing theme is War War - its stern realities & consequences Which at present our Country has begun to feel the Weight of considerably.

Editorial note Friday Jan 29th 1864 is the reading of the heading. Extensive dark photocopy interference obscures words; the social comparison in the lower half particularly needs review. The text continues in the upper upright block of scan page 5.

04-010-P03Dear Sir

All had been fill with sanguine emotions of gladness and joyous are the recollections of by gone times. Cousin George In this dark hour of bereavement you have my Sympathies. 04-012 · verse

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 12

04-012-P01The River of death
04-012-P02Beautiful world! I've trusted long To your gilded show, your Syren Song; I thought the joys you gave would last But one by one they have hurried past; Till now the last you could give has flown, And I stand by the River, Alone! Alone!
04-012-P03I was merry once, and to music sweet Kept tinkling time with dancing feet; I crowned my hair with roses red, But the lute is shatter'd, the roses dead; Music and blossoms are long since gone, And I stand by the River, Alone! Alone!
04-012-P04And once I loved; 'twas a dream of bliss, Could earth give more to her child than this? Rapture so great it was almost pain, A girdle of flowers with a silken chain; But love like a vision of night has flown, And I stand by the River, Alone! Alone!

Editorial note Manuscript copy of a poem. The title and verse lineation are retained. Continues on scan 13.

04-013 · verse and letter

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 13

04-013-P01Then madly I quaffed the cup of fame, And smiled when thousands lisp'd my name, My brow was bound with a wreath of bay, But it faded quickly and drooped away, Leaf after leaf until all are gone, And I stand by the River Alone! Alone!
04-013-P02And what have I done O Christ! for thee? Drear the visions my dim eyes see! Have I given a cup of water cold To a single Lamb in Thy precious fold? Time for working is past and gone, And I stand by the River Alone! Alone!
04-013-P03O Christ! have mercy! The waters wild Are sweeping away Thy erring child; Wilt Thou not listen? O, take my hand, And guide me through to a distant land; All that I trusted on earth has flown, Must I sink in the River, Alone! Alone!
04-013-P04The River! The River! tis dark and wide, But I see a light on the other Side! My heart no longer is faint with fear, For Heaven has help; my God is near! With joyous footsteps I hasten on No more in the River, Alone! Alone!
04-013-P05[[separate block: accompanying letter]]

04-013-P07Cousin Lizzie

I think them very beautiful — and this Express doubtless the position of every persons decease.
04-013-P09Your Cousin James
04-013-P10To Lizzie

04-017 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 17

04-017-P01[[Yellow?]] Branch [[illegible]] Sept 18th 1863
04-017-P02Cousin George. I have written Several times to you but Silence has been the result. My uncertain Whereabouts perhaps have been the Cause — for I cannot think you so ungenerous as not to have Written to me Since Sept last year. I have thought my letters of last Spring may have Contained something not acceptable — if so I cannot conceive what it was — for if you are like the majority of Soldiers — your Feelings could hardly be ruffled — I will try you another Effort and if Silence is the response I may know Something is wrong in Denmark, & will call off my hounds. But as I am floating in rather a dull atmosphere I will not promise to be very interesting but trust I may for a while relieve you of the monotony of the Camps.
04-017-P03I am home on a detail after Clothing for our Co. having reached Sat Evening. I find things moving on in this vicinity about like they did before the War began save the absence of Young men. Plenty Seems Smiling at every door — and were it not for absent ones I believe the people here would be happy. [[illegible]] quite warm weathering Carried on in North Carolina. As yet I have visited but Little. I have visited Waxahachie twice — a very important case being under Consideration in the Court — To wit; The murder of a Mrs Claunch by her Daughter — The case has not yet been decided but from the evidence I have heard believe she will come clear — but not in the Eyes of the people — as innocent of the offence. From Waxa on Monday I went to Red Oak Camp Ground — Camp meeting being on hand — and have remained till Tuesday — The meeting not so largely attended as in former years — Especially by the Sterner Sex — but there has been no discount on the other Side. The eating part received marked attention on my part. I indulge freely in those Substantials that keep Soul & body together — but did not Labor very hard to make myself agreeable — or to create a Contagious Sometimes Called a “palpitation of the heart” — My observation teaches me That the germs of this malady are in an [[illegible]] State and only need Sunshine to bring them to bloom, Expand & Captivate —
04-017-P04They had Shouting times that night — Some things done rather disorders — Some Singular movements. Amongst them was a Soldier friend — who yielded to the Solicitation of an Old Gentleman who has a Daughter that has [[illegible]] Claims upon him — I think the meeting is Still in operation — If Shoutings are an indication of Godliness — This is Certainly the most Christian people in the range of my knowledge. Possibly this may be attributable to the fact of the abundance of Preachers — who Claim exemption by reason of their vocation and fearing if they do not all Preach they will have to Shoulder their musket & Battle Some

Editorial note Letter in a small, closely written hand with a fold and scattered reproduction damage. The first word of the place-name and several clauses remain uncertain. Continues on scan 18.

04-018 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 18

04-018-P01I will now tell you Something of the operations of our Command. In June it was ordered West & have just returned to about where we! The [[illegible]] was Countermanded & it with another [[illegible]] returned to the vicinity of [[illegible]] Remained in the Same about a month recruiting our horses. About The 1st of Aug. We moved to the Miss. Valley near Natchez — remained there a Couple of Weeks & then were ordered to Our former field (Ark). On the 31st of Aug the Brigade Started on their way rejoicing. We had Seen the Elephant in La & the Boys were willing to go any where. They will doubtless See Some other large Animal in Ark for they will have to ride it as they Left it. [[deleted: illegible]] The Division will be home before there — and they will have a tough time of it with Genl Steele. Majors' Div is also gone to take the [[illegible]] of Walker's [[illegible]] — Had taken up line of march for [[illegible]] & it was believed they would follow also — So now you may listen to hear of Stirring times this fall.
04-018-P02Whilst Scouting on the Miss Our Squad turned over about 30 fine Horses their arms &c to the Yankee Sent [[illegible]] of them went down to New Orleans to get some Crackers. The others made their Escape Swimming & hiding in the [[weeds?]]. They were Completely Surprised at 3 oclk AM on the 28 of Aug — and a great Wonder all did not get [[killed?]]. Capt [[Vardeman?]] again Got into [[illegible]] Some Candy — He & the dismounted men are now at home [[illegible]] the election — I was not in the Regt yet & Some [[illegible]] of my mess but [[illegible]] had not come out on [[illegible]]. Bob got to Camps the day before I left — I had had chills for a month in the Summer was Sick last Evening & Expect them returning — Jeffie was down last week.
04-018-P03We Learn Lizzie spent Sunday with you & she Returned to John on Monday. [[Laura?]] Spent last night here. Mother has had a very Sick Spell is getting over it. Had a fine Rain day before yesterday. [[Br?]] Sam Austin Called in today Returning from the meeting — The same is Still going on [[illegible]] the Camp but has been dispersed with only 7 professions So far — We recently Recd a Letter from Lafayette of [[date?]] 1st of Aug. Their Brigade had a heavy fight on the 22nd of July — their Regt went into Battle with 320 & Came out with about 80 — James who was in the [[illegible]] had a narrow Escape [[illegible]] All are Stout & healthy — They are [[illegible]] in high hopes of Early End of the War. I hope their [[illegible]] may be realized.
04-018-P04[[margin]] Since [[illegible]] — Those of their Co that was not killed had to Surrender & while They were Stacking arms Hage [[illegible]] through [[illegible]] & Clem [[illegible]] was found at hind [[illegible]] My paper is full — I hope to hear from you before I leave for the army — Yours Truly H D [[/margin]]

04-019 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 19

04-019-P01Cold Springs Texas Sept 19th /63
04-019-P02Mr George Douglas
04-019-P03Dear cousin
04-019-P04Perhaps you will think strange of receiving a letter from me as you have in all probability forgotten your old hunting companion and school mate it has been A long time since we used to meet down on old Brushy and give the old gobblers a round. and I fear it will be a longer one Still before we can have that blessed privilege again. nearly Four years have pass Since I Saw you and I imagined that you are the Same old george you used to be. I have not changed in the least, only from the big to the bigger. I weigh 175 pounds and the largest Devil in co. (F.) I was ordered to report to the Brigade Quarter [[Mr?]] for fatigue duty this morning. got half way turned round and came back and now looking for the guard to arrest me and if they do I will lye out of it Some how.
04-019-P05We are having A very pleasant time at this camp duty easy Drill Four evry day except Sundays [[as wet foots?]] we also have a chain guard around our camp wont let a fellow out with his Saddle. you ought to See the boys riding all over the Country bare back There is a big meeting going on in camp. There was about forty mourners last night. They haven't got me yet but I dont know how Soon they will for evry time the old fellow preaches Scare [[there?]] is A [[frog?]] in my throat as big as your fist

Editorial note Letter continues on scan 20. Original informal spelling is retained. “as wet foots” and the wording before “is A” are difficult and may warrant revision.

04-021 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 21

04-021-P01Tupelo Mississippi Jan 25th/65
04-021-P02Dear Brother
04-021-P03As Brother Lafayette will Start home in a few days on furlough I will write you a few lines to let you know how I am getting along I was painfully wounded on the 22nd of July while Charging the enemys works The ball Struck me on the the left wrist and passed throw my hand my wrist Bones was badly fractured also the Bone of the middle finger I have Suffered a great deal with it about the middle of August I took the fever and for too months and for two months I were hardly able to get off of my bunk with out assistance — I Received 60 days furlough in October to go to mobile to See our relatives — When I arrived at Demopolis my hand was paining me very much and I was very unwell So I Concluded to Stop a few days untill I could get better I Stoped with an old Lady By the name of Brassfield Thair I had all the attention a confederate Soldier Could wish for. By the [[time?]] I was able to go on I had Concluded [[to spend?]] my furlong in that neighborhood & go to Mobile

Editorial note The repeated “the” and repeated “for … months” are in the manuscript and are retained. The last two lines are crowded and partially obscured. Continues on scan 22.

04-022 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 22

04-022-P01Some other time. I spent the time very pleasantly & only Regreted that I Couldent Stay longer I Shall never forget the kind people of Ala.
04-022-P02At the expiration of my furlough I Started to the Command. But learning that I Couldent get to it. I turned my course and went to Columbus Miss. with the intention of getting my furlough extended and go on to Mobile. Before I Could get off from that place the Federals Destroyed the R Road So that I Could not get thair So I went in to the Hospital and remained thair untill a few days ago.
04-022-P03My hand is not well yet. it has healed up on both Sides untill the Sear is the Size of a pea and it not heal any more it is running all the time The Doctors Say that is Caused by deceased Bone or by Some pieces of Bone that aught to have been removed My wrist will be entirely Stiff I think I will have the use of all of my fingers except the middle one.
04-022-P04I Returned to the Command last week found the Boys all in good health Thair is very few of our Brigade left only about 4 hundred out of five Regiments

Editorial note Continuation of scan 21. “deceased Bone” is retained as the writer appears to have written it; no medical correction is substituted.

04-027 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 27

04-027-P01Monday Morning.
04-027-P02Dear Zella,
04-027-P03Of course you would like to know of the meeting. While there has been no further additions, yesterday was a “big” day. According to announcement there was dinner on the ground. And such another dinner. About the finest ever spread at Sylvana. Among visitors present was White Stirman and [[deleted: illegible]] wife, (who sends her love to you) and Nellie Russell. They left after preaching last night for Ennis, White and family going to move right away to Marlin, where White has a job of running a “Watkins” Medicine wagon.
04-027-P04From Oak Cliff were three carloads of people, Jeff Barclay among the number who kindly remembered you, the wife of J. C. Estes, the preacher, Bro. Stevens, mother wife and sister, Bro. Melton and family and Bro. Holsenback, and wife.

Editorial note Continuation on scan 28. Personal names are transcribed as read; no attempt has been made to identify these visitors from outside records.

04-028 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 28

04-028-P01These enjoyed themselves finely, and were profuse in their invitations for me to visit them when in Dallas.
04-028-P02Mrs. Dr. Mitchell and family, and Bro. Dunn's family were out from Waxahachie. These comprise the major part of the visitors who took dinner. After dinner Mrs Howard and family came in and remained until after night preaching. She was showered with attentions by all who knew her while she was with us, and last Cliff Stanford came much to the delight of his many friends.
04-028-P03The meeting may close tonight, and maybe tomorrow night. Bro. Dunn being something of a revivalist is blue over his work here, he is disappointed.
04-028-P04No change to report in Lucile's condition but I expect to finish this letter at Red Oak this evening; and will give latest dates.
04-028-P05Larkin is much worse; the disease taking a malignant form day before yesterday. I will hear from him this morning. All company has been forbidden. The prospect is most serious.

Editorial note Continuation of scan 27.

04-029 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 29

04-029-P01We will have to hedge on expenses. The Ellis Co. cotton crop is a failure today the eleventh of August. The worms have completely destroyed all the bolls and squares, and blooms are scarcer than I ever saw — if anything is made it has to be made in the future, there is nothing now. Worms are of all sizes and this argues that they will be with us some time yet.
04-029-P02I hope you will continue to do well, get some weight and strength and hold both. I am feeling fine, batching must agree with me; but I am not stuck on it.
04-029-P03Am getting from two to eight eggs per day.
04-029-P04At Kate's. I found Lucile not so well as last Sunday — She seems quite unwell, suffering considerably. Kate says: “Her stomach has gone back on her.” Poor Child, she looks badly. Larkin is about the same as [[illegible]] (Saturday) If he has strength to stand it he may pull through all right.

Editorial note Continuation of scan 28. The pencil postscript is much fainter than the ink text. One word or phrase after “same as” remains unread.

04-030 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 30

04-030-P01Sermon to the Church to day — Ila Duncan and Mayfaire made the confession and will be baptized this evening — meeting will close tomorrow night.
04-030-P02Came up today and had a real civilized dinner with [[Bros?]] Duncan. I dont see why all women can't cook. Its so much nicer —
04-030-P03Jesse Ligon and family have returned to Red Oak, bag and baggage. I am glad they have come back as Jess is the best Ligon of the whole bunch — Did I tell you that the old wooden center of Red Oak burned out from brick to brick? Well the old rat traps are gone. Some loss heavily —
04-030-P04This covers the ground to date — Affectionately G. D.
04-030-P05Do the best you can for yourself while you are there. Dont bother about me. My peach preserves were highly praised

04-032 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 32

04-032-P01Wednesday Eve
04-032-P02Dear Zella, Just got your letter. I staid with Clarence & Kate Sunday night and saw you in the car window Monday morning as you passed.
04-032-P03Came on home in the evening, and today went back for the laundry and the young lady. Had a puncture coming home and got mighty hot and dirty fixing it. Delivered the young lady at her home and in a few hundred yards returning the same wheel went down again. Had a time. Some men came along and all of us put together could not fix it, so I drove to Rockett on the rim, and with the help of Sid Farrar soon had it righted up. Came on and spent three hours with Kate. The children surely look bad. Glenn was not well, but Kate looks much better than I expected.

Editorial note Letter continues on scan 33.

04-033 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 33

04-033-P01They will have to give up that place the first of August. Will likely come to Roland's house in the pasture.
04-033-P02The letter to you from Paris I opened to see if it should be forwarded. Find it enclosed. Will send yours to your Aunt Nannie by way of explanation.
04-033-P03I do hope your appetite will stay with you and that the girls will see to your wants. Dont be a burden and dont overdo yourself.
04-033-P04Dont worry about me. I boiled me two potato's and six eggs yesterday evening, and Ruth gave me a glass of plum jelly and I have plenty of bread, and I reboiled the Molasses and strained it through the new sifter. So I am prepared to get fat. Elmer Carter is very glad to get all of the Cow. Rain plenty since you left. Tell Edwina I enjoyed reading her good letter hugely.
04-033-P05Affectionately G. D.

Editorial note Closing continuation of scan 32. “get all of the Cow” is retained as written; the wording has not been expanded into an inferred meaning.

04-034 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 34

04-034-P01[[deleted: Monday]] [[inserted: Tuesday]] Morning.
04-034-P02Dear Zella —
04-034-P03Yours written Sunday came yesterday. I am glad you did not have a bad time last week, and hope you will do better this week. I know Ed's expenses must be enormous; but be sure you do not add any to them. Our house keepers packed up their goods Saturday, and Sunday Evening loaded them on two wagons, and Monday morning pulled out for Bartlett Texas. At Waxahachie they shipped their effects, and by now are at their destination.
04-034-P04So my prospects for baching grows brighter from day to day. Sure I live better, and my next cook must look refined. My nieces cant understand why I persist in staying at home, and there is not much use for it except I hate for the house to be unoccupied at night. Elmer Carter has taken the Cow to his house, and there is nothing to do but gather up a very few eggs of an evening.

Editorial note The weekday was corrected above the original “Monday.” Continuation on scan 35.

04-035 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 35

04-035-P01The hens are on strike mostly. They mainly water just below the Feterita patch at a swamp spring, and the feterita furnishes them fine picking.
04-035-P02Our meeting crowds are small, and the preaching not much bigger. It chafes Bro. Dunn very perceptibly, but he has a poor way of helping himself, as the job of building up a large audience is a big one. The crowds will be apt to grow later on.
04-035-P03Word came down last night that Olaf wished to be baptized, so Bro. Duncan returned immediately to Red Oak to make preparation and Bro. Dunn and others went up after Church was over to attend to it. I have no word from them up to this writing. Dr. Stoker told the friends that apparently he had [[deleted: only]] only a few days to live. This is hard on Kate but not unexpected. Lucile can sit up in a rocker an hour or so each day. I will close this when I hear from them after awhile.
04-035-P04At Kate's Tuesday Eve. Olaf is resting fairly well now. Looks bad, and very much emaciated. Plenty of friends here, which [[inserted: makes]] them feel that they are not neglected. Do not note any change in Lucile —
04-035-P05Affectionately G. D.

Editorial note Closing continuation of scan 34. A repeated canceled word and an insertion in the pencil postscript are recorded.

04-036 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 36

04-036-P01Thursday Morning
04-036-P02Dear Zella,
04-036-P03Olaf died yesterday evening at six thirty. I am just ready to start up there. Was there a couple of hours yesterday morning. They had all the attention from the outside that was needful for their comfort.
04-036-P04Lucile is still able to sit up a little while every day.
04-036-P05Larkin is sick with [[deleted: bl]] blood poisoning. Was there a couple of hours yesterday evening. To begin with a hen scratched him on the thumb some days ago, this inflamed and was very painful. That got some better and now he has a large red spot on top of his right foot with red streaks running most up to the knee, he has some fever but is not suffering much. Mrs. Wadley, the Dr's mother died yesterday evening.

Editorial note Letter continues on scan 37. The partial canceled start of “blood” is recorded.

04-037 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 37

04-037-P01Red Oak Thursday Evening — Just back from Olof's burial at the Rawlins cemetery — Many friends and sympathizers were present. Bro Dunn made a good comforting talk at the tabernacle. Twas very hot but I stood it pretty well — I cannot say what effect all of this death and funeral will have on Lucile yet — She seems to have considerable vitality and may linger for quite awhile — Hattie Stanford is to be baptized this evening. Quite a lot of the Patrick Chapel people were down today, and quite a lot are reported coming from Oak Cliff Sunday —
04-037-P02I hope you are doing finely this week.
04-037-P03Affectionately G. D.

Editorial note Closing continuation of scan 36. The name spelled “Olaf” on scan 36 appears as “Olof” here; the difference is retained.

04-038 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 38

04-038-P01Thursday Morning.
04-038-P02Dear Zella,
04-038-P03I am still Baching and doing fine. Somehow I do not expect my housekeepers any way soon.
04-038-P04Mary Roberts sent down and got all the Fruit jars she wanted some days ago, and yesterday Evening Edna came and got seventeen for her Mother.
04-038-P05I got some of the peaches from the tree up by the mustang vine, and yesterday I put up a gallon and a quart of preserves all by myself. I had 12 pounds of peaches, and 9 pounds sugar, began at sun up and got through by noon. I did not use a single drop of water. Have disposed of 13 dollars worth of potato's and have plenty left. Kate and children are doing as well as could be expected.
04-038-P06Nothing new. Affectionately G. D.

04-053 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 53

04-053-P01I would love for you to be here, for I get very blue and lonesome at times, and you are all I have that is best to live for. With the number of my years I cant stay here long, so your company is that much more precious. I may survive this trouble and may not. At all events my physical strength will be greatly weakened, and for this reason I wanted the car sold, as my car cranking days are over.

Editorial note Additional page associated with the September 3 letter. No page number is visible.

04-065-P01Wednesday 11-23- My Dear Child. Yours of Monday just to hand and I am glad of the favorable report. Maybe we will have enough to "get by" on. The healing process is doing nicely, the only trouble is I have to keep my jaw still, and this cuts out the Eating act to two meals a day. I told the Dr. I was willing to cut it out to every two or three days if it would help matters. My general health is as good as it ever was.

04-066-P012 Now as to the old house. I have got to tighten up my room for this winter. I noticed last winter that the cold air came in very perceptibly through the ceiling joints. There is three ways to stop this. Ceil over the old ceiling with new lumber, paper or paint the walls. I put it up to you to fix it, as I may not get home in time to do it before cold weather. The front of the house needs repainting, and it would be the quickest

04-079-P01Palestine, Sept. 8th 1851 Dear Father, Your letter of August 16th came to hand a few days ago, and I was much gratified to hear from you all again. I have just been writing to sister Rebecca, and scolded a good deal with [[her?]] for none of you writing to me, but I see from your letter that the fault lay entirely with the mail, so I shall not send it. I am glad to hear of the good health of the Family, and it gives me some hopes that health may be enjoyed in Brushy, as well as other places. This County although reputed to be one of the healthiest in the State, has been very sickly for two months past, more so than I ever saw it in Ellis Co. I think that Old Mr Benson is nearly willing to try the [[illegible]] again for he has not bettered himself so far as health is concerned, by moving down here. There has been a great many deaths in the county, & five Doctors living in this place have had their hands full of practice, tho' now it is becoming [[margin]] more healthy [[/margin]]

Editorial note Heavy show-through from the reverse interferes with several readings. The place name following “enjoyed in” reads Brushy after image review.

04-080 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 04 - Folder 1, Correspondence and Loan Papers.pdf · Scan page 80

04-080-P01My own health never was better than it has been since I have been in this place. I still get plenty of work to do. I quit working for Mr Dale about a month ago & have been picking up little jobs round ever since. Were it not so expensive to live here I could make a tolerably fair living. I am now helping build a large Livery Stable, my wages are $1,50 cts pr day, but after subtracting $10 pr month for board & washing, the amount is considerably diminished. There is going to be a very good opening for carpenters here this coming Winter and Spring, and I am somewhat at a loss whether to continue journeywork or turn out undertaker, I think I could get plenty to do either way. The Christian Church at this place have resolved to build a house of worship as soon as lumber can be procured. It will be 56 ft long & 34 wide and will present a very neat appearance when finished. It has not rained here for so long that I forget when it did rain last, every thing is very much parched up. Corn crops are tolerably good, but Cotton will not make more than a half crop

Editorial note Image review reads the verb build before “a large Livery Stable”; the earlier proposed surname has been withdrawn. Show-through is visible throughout.

04-083-P01Jan. 12 1862 Dear Sister Lizzie I received your letter last week. Beck was here and we had the pleasure of reading it together it had been sometime since we had herd from you. we looked for some of you up Christmas. I had plenty of cake and pies at my cabin Christmas day and a big Egg nog. I envited some of the near neighbors to come and dine with me. and about ten oclock I looked out and saw 14 young men and girls riged out comeing I did not know that they were comeing so you see they stormed me. you may depend the youngsters about here had a lively time of it all the week after which most all the young men left for the war. I wish you and Laura had been here. I have not been any where since christmas week for I have been sick ever since was not well for a week before I am chilling now and have the old [[razzle?]] as heath calls it. [[Gaskin?]] had a chill last week and now has very sore eyes he is walking and is as pretty as any bodys boy he can talk now as good as Angus. Maggie is growing fast and will be another Sister Fleming

Editorial note Personal names and the colloquial expression before “as heath calls it” should be checked against the original.

04-093-P01Palestine Dec. 3d 1853 Dear Mother Time passes rapidly away, & tonight I find myself writing to you instead of being on my road home, as I had wished I often think that it is not worth while to make anything like definite calculations for the future; as I fail, in them so often. The time of my going home seems to be so uncertain that I shall make no promises about it this time. The principal reason of my delay is, that I have, incurred more expences than I anticipated, & shall have to work longer in consequence. I hoped to see Warren, down here last week, but was disappointed I had however the pleasure of meeting with David Gaslin, at Bonners ferry on Trinity, last monday evening & also on Tuesday morning. Kendrick had all the preaching to do. There were [[6?]] baptized & one other added to the Church 2 of them were little boys, one, Addison, son of Bro Clark aged about 12 years the other Albert, Bro. K's oldest son about 10 years old. They seemed fully to understand (& I have no doubt did) what they were doing. It was a beautiful & interesting scene to see children, young & comparatively innocent, pressing into the Kingdom, & thus consecrating their lives to the Redeemer Whilst others, old & gray, could sit unmoved, under the appeals of the Gospel, nor be persuaded to devote the little remainder of their 3 score & ten, to the only Cause worth living for, & the only one that promises aught for the future

Editorial note The baptism count is blotted. Fragments of another sheet visible behind the right edge are not a continuous readable passage.

05-002-P01To N. L. Douglas from Henry Douglas Palestine, Tex. Jan. 12, 1855

05-003 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 3

05-003-P01Palestine Tex. Jan. 12 1855 Dear Father: I have been looking for a letter from home; for a long time, not having heard from any of you since I left there in Oct. But as I have not heard I [[illegible]] to write again [[illegible]] I think it rather uncertain [[illegible]] you would hear from me Lest you might have failed to get any of my late letters, I would say that I have written several times since November, & this must excuse me if such be the case I much desired a letter from you before making arrangements for the present year, & I waited until New Years day for that purpose. Being then left entirely on my own judgement I concluded to remain here one year longer, & risk the consequences. Dr Kendrick & myself have formed a partnership for the year. I get ⅓ of the accounts, & am at ⅓ the expence, pay my board & furnish my own horse The advantages of this arrangement are, — his experience — a share in a practice which is firmly established — and a division of responsibility in bad cases, which is not the least of things to be considered. The disadvantages are,

Editorial note Abrasion or fading at the folds interrupts the opening paragraph. The shares are read as one-third; the second is faint.

05-004 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 4

05-004-P01— a probability of making more money elsewhere — the greater incentive to study, by being thrown on my own resources — & it may be the loss of a good field of operations by being 1 year later. — Have I done wisely, or not? We have great opposition here both from [[numbers?]] & prej[[illegible]] This latter is, however giving [[illegible]] in the minds of the people; & as for the Doctors, it makes but little difference what they think, say, or do. Dr K's practice has been regularly on the increase, for some time, & will continue to do so. Old Mr Ransom, (whom I take to be a man of strong prejudices) has declared his intention to try Dr K when next he wants a physician. Others, the same. I mention him, as he is known to you I wrote to Warren, of a Cancer case we have on hand, which was considered doubtful, & is probably so yet, but it is now doing well & we have more hope. It was a great risk, but will be a great triumph, if a cure is effected. — My health is good. I think I can venture to promise you a visit in the Spring, say April. I could come sooner, but the winter winds & sleets are too bad. If you have not written please do so immediately. My love to all Yours in all affection Henry Douglas N. L. Douglas

Editorial note Two faded phrases near the top remain unresolved. Medical claims are transcribed as the writer’s historical statements.

05-005 · identifying slip

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 5

05-005-P01To Mrs. Mary Douglas from Henry Douglas Palestine, Texas April 8, 1853

05-006 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 6

05-006-P01Palestine Tex. Ap. 8, 1853 Dear Mother: The letter from yourself & Susannah, came by last mail, & lest you be somewhat disappointed by my delay in my promised visit, I now drop you a line I recd a letter from Uncle Sam a short time ago, suggesting the 1st of May, as the most suitable time for me to come up, in consequence of his engagements, at court in April & also Jepie's & Warrens absences, he, thinking it probable that they would not be at home until about that time. As it was of no material difference to me, I wrote him immediately that I would be up the last week in April. & lest the letter should miscarry, I write this. Your letter was nearly 3 weeks on the way & if this makes no better speed, & nothing should hinder, I will be with you soon after you get it. I am pleased that Susannah has consented to come down with me, & hope there will be no disappointment, in any way; but that the step shall prove entirely satisfactory to all concerned. I think you are disposed to praise my humble self entirely too highly in this matter; for I have only done, what seemed to me, a reasonable duty. I feel that I have been blessed beyond my expectations, in whatsoever

05-007 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 7

05-007-P01I have laid my hand to, since I left your roof to seek my fortune in the wide world. I know that while memory lasts I shall never forget the day when I parted from Father, in the wide lonely prairie, near the Willow pond, his parting advice, & the loneliness of the subsequent hours in walking to Porters Bluff. Never till then had I felt so isolated; never had I felt what it was to be homeless, houseless, & in effect, friendless, for I was going amongst strangers, & I knew not whither I took everything upon trust; & have not been disappointed. I have been blest with health & plenty; not so abundantly as some, but as much as I had any right to hope for. I repeat then, that I deserve no praise, but if I can render any service to others I am only doing my duty. You may, then, expect me up about the last of April, (of course I can't say precisely) & I hope to remain with you a week or 10 days, maybe longer. The weather has been very dry with us, for a long time, & crops are suffering greatly for rain. Corn has been badly frostbitten & much of it will be to plant again. I fear the wheat has been ruined, with you. I remain as ever, your affectionate Son, Henry Douglas. Mrs Mary Douglas
05-007-P02[[margin]] P.S. Give my love to all. Remember me to [[Un.?]] & aunt Cynthia. &c &c H.D. [[/margin]]

Editorial note The postscript is inverted at the top of the image. The abbreviation before “& aunt Cynthia” is uncertain.

05-008 · identifying slip

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 8

05-008-P01To N. L. Douglas from Henry Douglas Mississippi County, Missouri June 23, 1860 — re driving herd of sheep to Texas

05-009 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 9

05-009-P01Missisipi County, Missouri June 23d 1860 Dear Father: After a long lapse of time I now find an opportunity to write you. I have nothing encouraging to write. I have had to pay from 1 to 2 dollars for sheep & have been at a great deal of trouble to find them & expense in collecting my drove. Mr Willis Helm sent $500 by me to get him some sheep & with the 1500 I have bought 750 head & do not know that I will have money enough to get them to Tex. as I only have some 200 left. I bought common sheep in Callaway & Graves Co's Ky purchase lambs at 1 & Ewes at 1.25 per head but could not get enough & so went back to Christian Co & got some 250 head at 1.75 & 2 per head These are finer sheep & I would now prefer that all my funds were invested in such sheep I crossed the Mississippi at the Iron Banks last Sunday & had to pay 8 cts per head ferriage then after driving two days I had to stop on account of the sheep taking a disease from poison or some thing else. Several have died & altogether I have lost near 50 head since I started, mostly however by their dodging in the thickets the first few days

05-010 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 10

05-010-P01driving. I learn that every drove of sheep that has passed through here has suffered severely in the same way that mine has. Last year about this time a Mr Long passed through with 1000 & lost 150 to 200. A Mr South, Methodist preacher, with 3 or 400 lost only a few. He passed through about the last of July. A few days ago a drove of 7 or 800 passed from Rutherford Co Tennessee & lost a great many. The people here say that I have escaped better than any of them & they say, further, that after leaving here all the droves did well which I greatly hope is true. I now have my sheep in a large crabgrass pasture free of charge, & will remain till I think it prudent to start which I hope will be in 3 or 4 days. Nearly all are doing finely but probably 50 show a diseased condition. The symptoms are, first a drooping & swelling of the ears, which thicken up the cellular tissue being filled with yellow serum. The sheep becomes dull & stupid & in some cases the head swells — under the jaw the skin will hang in a pouch & I have stuck my knife into several & the yellow serum would flow freely. I know that I have lost 5 since I crossed the river & one more will die, probably. I forget to say that, after the head swells the sheep suffers from an intolerable itching, judging from its actions, & the hair will come off when the swelling goes. I have 2 toll gates

05-011 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 11

05-011-P01to pass before I get clear of the swamps & then no more I suppose till I get to Fort Gibson ferry on the Arkansas. I have 4 hands hired at 15 dollars per month each & taking all together I cannot make the drove profitable if I do not lose by it. I have not heard from any of you since I left Texas nor have I heard from Ellen since I left Lincoln County. Taking everything into consideration I cannot feel otherwise than "low down" but win or lose I am bound to go through. My health is still good & if I dont get sick I think I can stand it. Now I have written this I do not know that it would be best to send it, but you had better know the truth than to be in suspense. I suppose I will reach Springfield Mo. in 30 or 40 days & you must write me at that place & also at Fort Gibson, & write to Warren to do the same as I am very anxious to hear from all of you. I have been much disappointed & perplexed by men who engaged with me for the trip, failing to come up at the appointed time & some not coming at all. This required me to hire hands temporarily & higher prices & to pay them off when they quit, which has been a heavy draw on my pocket. I will write again — for the present this is enough. Love to all. Your affectionate son. H. Douglas

05-014-P01Murfreesboro Ten April 18 /54. My Dear Sir It has been about two years since I heard from you in relation to our business — it has been I believe nearly three years. Sublett owns it & I am thinking about buying it. I believe the Land is at the mouth of the five mile [[deleted: branch?]] [[inserted: creek]] Write me what the Land is worth & give me any information you may have, or can get, in relation to it.

05-036-P01Bardsdale, Sat, 29th, 1893.
05-036-P02My Dear Aunt:—I finished up my morning work pretty well this forenoon, so now I will take time to write to you. I am still better—haven’t had a “break down” since I wrote last.
05-036-P03I have every room swept nicely—I still have a bed in my sitting-room two rockers, two footstools, nicely covered, the stand-table, Mr. [[Curren?]] made, two tea-baskets on the mantle-piece, my vases, & two advertisement pictures, which makes the room presentable.

05-047 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 47

05-047-P01Bardsdale August 8th. Dearest Aunt:—I have just gotten dinner over. Mr. Headrick has gone to work, Elmo is seated on the table, and Bruce by it with a slate, and talking more. I am as well as usual. Mr. H. helped me wash yesterday. Grandma Humphrey’s growth increases, and is so bad she has difficulty in breathing—consequently has consented to have an operation performed; which, I greatly fear she will not undergo. Dr. [[Hinckley?]] is back now and he Drs. Bard and Mott, are to do the work. I saw Dr. H. passing, once since his return. I spoke, told him I was glad to see him back, and expressed lasting gratitude for his kindness. Could hardly keep from saying if he thought half as much of me, as I did of him, he would have called to see us sooner. but of course we can’t express ourselves thus freely, to strangers.
05-047-P02We had our picnic at Mr. Grimes, last saturday, which was quite a success. I prepared a good dinner in case I should get a chance to go; but the hour came for starting, and no opportunity presented itself. How fully I realized that if I was back there, or even you here it would be different. Had I acted on my own feelings, I would have put forth no effort to go; but the children were so anxious to go, they said, if I didn’t go down and ask Mr. Wigmore for his cart, they would. I knew they couldn’t bring it alone, so I went with them but fortunately for my “brazen face” it had been spoken for, and I found it out before I had to ask. I found Mrs. Pickering hardly able to be out of bed, and still fixing to go off camping, in hopes ’twould enable her to stay a little while longer with her children. So I swept her house, cooked her two jelly cakes, and came on home about noon. Mr. H. took his dinner so the children and I sat down and ate picnic dinner alone. After we had finished, and I had sat down, to read a little, down came Mr. Henry Chadsey in Mrs. [[Curren’s?]] surrey, for us. Lillie Chadsey had inquired of several why I wasn’t there, and

Editorial note No year is supplied in this page’s heading. Several personal names have uncertain letter forms.

05-048 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 48

05-048-P01the answer of all was of course, “I guess she didn’t have any way”. She told them there was no reason I shouldn’t have a way, when there were so many conveyances there, so she started Henry in a hurry. I was thankful on account of the children especially. They had a large table heavily loaded, with choicest food. Mrs. Grimes took up nearly all her dishes, and every one else took plenty so it was a set table just like you would have at your own home. But just such a mixture of dishes, you never heard of. It took them quite awhile to get them straightened out. They had lemonade and ice-cream. two swings a set of croquet, and a game of baseball. And [[no part?]] of the pleasure was due wholly to Lillie Chadsey. I was amused to observe, as she came down in the afternoon, to see Elmo running her. Imagine him handling (or rather attempting to) over Two hundred pounds. But you know he always rather liked her. That was the 4th, and little [[Beckie’s?]] birthday, and what think you, she went to Santa Paula with Mr. [[Reck?]] [[Lehock?]] and married him unbeknowing to her father. The old gentleman is quite wrathy over it. ordered her off the place, when she went back for her clothes, and threatened to disinherit her. It was her 18th birthday. The [[Wilmot?]] [[Hagarts?]] are not all dead yet. Grandma Roberts spent last sunday night with us. I am always so glad to see her. I forgot to say, I heartily wished for you to dine with us, when we sat down to eat our picnic dinner all alone. Elmo said to me one day “when a girl marries a young man, she takes his name, doesn’t she”, to which I said yes, and he continued how many Headricks will there be then? Bruce was standing by, and put in, I’m going to marry a “dutch girl”. Well I must tell you that I earned $4.50 pitting fruit. A handsome amount for me.
05-048-P02Expecting an account of the meeting soon, I am as ever, S. G. H.

Editorial note The phrase before “of the pleasure” appears to read “no part”; it is marked as tentative. Personal names remain uncertain. The earnings are for pitting fruit.

05-066 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 66

05-066-P01In answer to the Author of “Georgie.”
05-066-P02I seek not for devoted dears.— “Platonic friendship at my years,” Most surely will suffice, Impassioned hours have long gone by— The furtive glance—the pensive sigh— [[Was as for lovers nice?]]. Give me a strong and helping hand While adverse clouds hang low, A friend in danger’s hour, who’ll stand Then onward will I go I’ll meet cold fate, with heart elate And smile at fortune’s frown, I’ll bare my breast, to its rudest blast, Altho’ I may go down,
05-066-P03“Beauties newblown” will Nature bring With each returning vernal Spring And I enjoy these, The flowrets on the prairies wide The murmuring streams which sweetly glide The grand and lofty trees, Altho’ I am an Anchorite Absolved from humankind Some joys will stop with me o’er night And leave a pledge behind, That in after years, thro’ changeful cares They’ll call around once more And bring with them, some nameless gem “From the distant Aiden shore,
05-066-P04Thus Hope is still the harbinger Of many a blissful coming year Which fortune may deny.

Editorial note The sixth line remains a tentative reading. No substitute has been imported from another version of the poem. The closing quotation after “shore” is not visible.

05-078 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 78

05-078-P01Of all the thoughts enclosed in the language of Inspiration [[lost: left edge]] its laws its precepts its promises what is there that conveys more, to our minds and hearts than is expressed in this short sentence? Jesus wept. Let us look a moment at the character of Jesus: who, and what is he? If we accept the truths of the Bible, he is the son of God, The King of the Jews, The bright and morning star! A ruler of the Jews came to Jesus and said, "we know thou art a teacher come from God for no man can do the miracles that thou doest, except God be with him." Look then at the power of this man: by his word water is turned into wine. The blind receive their sight, the lame walk. By His word the tempest is stilled, and He walks the waves of the wild Galilee. By His word the dead are called back to life again. Truly He was the son of God.
05-078-P02But it is not from the Bible alone that we have testimony in regard to the greatness of this man. Renan in his Life of Christ says "All ages will proclaim that among the sons of men there were none born greater than Jesus." His goodness, [[greatness?]], his omnipotent power, we cannot doubt. We stand in awe as we contemplate them, and our hearts acknowledge him as King of Kings and Lord of Lords. But while we contemplate all of this glorious majesty our eye falls upon this simple sentence Jesus wept. What do we learn by this? by these two short words. We learn that He had a heart that could be touched by the sorrows and Suffering of our frail humanity: that Sympath[[lost: torn edge]]

Editorial note Also reproduced in source 02, scan page 3. Damaged line beginnings and the torn final word remain unresolved. Continues on scan page 79.

05-079 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 79

05-079-P01the power to "make e'en the wretched blessed" had its abode in His breast and that He could be touched by a feeling of our infirmities. How our hearts thrill with emotions of Love and thankfulness when we think of this blessed truth, that He was in sympathy with the sorrows of frail humanity.
05-079-P02More than eighteen hundred years have been measured by the revolving Earth since Jesus walked the shores and waves of Galilee, but the influence of His words and life, are still felt. And down through the vista of the coming Ages they will be felt till time shall be no more. And with all the records of His wonderful life this simple truth will descend with the others, Jesus wept.
05-079-P03L. Douglas. Sylvana Feb 16th 1883
05-079-P04Forever reign victorious King Wide through the earth thy name be known; And call our longing Souls to sing, Sublimest anthems near thy throne.

05-080 · label

05-080-P01The Beauty of Culture by Sarah Douglas

05-081 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 81

05-081-P01The beauty of female culture. There is nothing more important than the cultivation of our minds there is given to each of us a mind some superior to others but it matters not what degree of intellect we possess it is the common duty of all to improve it If we do not do this what will be the result? why it will be as a field that is not in cultivation and grows up in bryars and weeds.
05-081-P02The greater mind we possess the more we are expected to accomplish. I think there is more in the cultivation of the mind than there is in the mind itself.
05-081-P03We must also cultivate the heart kindness of feelings and gentleness of disposition.
05-081-P04There is also a great importance attached to the cultivation of our manners for what makes a young lady appear to better advantage than gracefulness and politeness of manners all true politeness originates in kindness of heart for if we feel kindly towards anyone then it is no trouble to act kindly to them.
05-081-P05Then let us all [[deleted: illegible]] earnestly strive to accomplish this so that we may make ourselves equal with those that now so far surpass us.
05-081-P06Sarah E. Douglas April [[21?]].

Editorial note Essay. Date is faint; no year is visible.

05-083 · label

05-083-P01Composition “Parting from Friends” by Emma Bell

05-084 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 84

05-084-P01Parting From Friends O! how sad we feel when we part from our dear friends! This is the case when we part a short time only, and expect to meet again soon, but how much [[inserted: illegible]] sad it is to part with them when we know that we will meet them no more in this world! Who can discribe the feelings of them who stand around the death bed of a loved father or mother, brother or sister, and hear from their dying lips the last fond words of love they will ever be permitted to utter. And then how agonizing to follow their lifeless bodies, and see them placed in the cold, and silent grave. No one can imagine the feelings caused by such a trial [[deleted: expept]] [[inserted: except]] those who have experienced it. All should be prepared to die, for we know not how soon we may be called upon to leave this beautiful world, and all our dear friends and loved ones. We should ever be kind to our relatives and friends for we do not know how soon they may be taken away from us, and then it will be a sad parting, indeed if we have ever treated them with unkindness.
05-084-P02[[separate block: annotation below essay]] Very good

Editorial note The correction above “sad” is not securely readable. “Very good” appears below the essay in a different-looking hand.

05-087 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 87

05-087-P01Murfreesboro Tenn Dec. 6–1883. Mrs. Douglas and Mrs. Orr. Ferris, Texas. Dear Friends: I send you an advertisement of the land sale which explains itself. During the Term of the Court, Chancellor Burton was sick and died. While he was sick a Circuit Court Judge held his Court and decided the case as to who the land goes to, but seemed very much tangled in the case.
05-087-P02He decided that 180 Acres of the land that descended to Mrs. Alexander from her father, Jacob Wright, goes to or is inherited by the nephews and nieces of Jacob Wright, and to the children of his deceased nephews and nieces, or their representatives, and as he had about forty nephews and nieces, seventeen of whom are living, and about twenty three dead would make about forty shares into which the estate would be divided. As to the McGlothlin tract of 230 Acres, descended to Mrs. Alexander from her mother, to use his language he said this tract “ascends” to the heirs of Mary Wright from her daughter, the said Rebecca, and the father and mother being cousins, that the heirs of the father and mother of Mary Wright, related to her in equal degree, or representing those in equal degree, take equal moities of said tract of 235 Acres – if such heirs or those they represent

Editorial note Continues on the following scan. The tract acreage appears as 230 and later 235 on this page; both readings are retained.

05-088 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 88

05-088-P01do not stand in equal degree to the said Mary Wright then the heirs nearest in blood to the said Mary, or them representing those nearest in blood to her of the whole and half blood will take the whole of said 230 Acres in preference to those of more remote degree.” I appealed from the whole decree to the Supreme Court as also did the McGlothlin claimants of the 230 Acre tract. I am in no way discouraged by the decision of the Court, and feel satisfied that I am correct in my construction of the law that Mrs. Alexander’s first cousins, living at the time of her death, inherit her whole estate from her, and that it does not ascend to the heirs of the Father, or Mother. About the time the case will be finally ended in the Supreme Court, the land money will be ready for distribution. Very Respectfully, B. F. Lillard. Per Ida.
05-088-P02Criminal and Chancery Courts to day are both in session and I have no time to write. B. F. Lillard

Editorial note Continuation of 05-087. Final note and signature are in a different hand from the body, which is marked “Per Ida.”

[[inserted: also for possession of the 640 Acre tract hereafter referred to]] If possession is not given on demand an ejectment suit will become necessary. 05-098 · manuscript

05-098-P01Murfreesboro Tenn. March 22nd 1883 Mr Geo. Douglas Ferris Texas – Dear Sir – Your letter of March 15th has been recd. I think that you have been under a misapprehension about several things – In the first place you certainly must think that after I recover these lands that I want to handle the money that may arise from the sale – In this you are mistaken I simply wanted to represent you in the litigation (I mean your Mother & Aunt) I never draw the funds of a party unless specially requested to do so – it is too much trouble to keep receipts &c, but then at the same time I want you to understand that I am thank God far ahead in this world’s goods, and I don’t know of a human being to whom I owe a cent, or whom I ever wronged, You do not understand the law in this State applicable to Attorneys – If an Attorney collects money and fails to pay it over his License [[deleted: was]] [[inserted: is]] taken from him, he is forever prohibited from practicing law, fined a thousand dollars, and imprisoned in jail 12 months, If you remember my first letter to [[deleted: you]] [[inserted: the ladies]] I stated that a letter instructing me to represent them was sufficient, but the Powers of Attorney that I sent was a more formal way and was preferable, and that I did not care about them acknowledging it [[inserted: before a clerk]] [[deleted: illegible]] your first suggested the acknowledgement, showing that

05-100 · manuscript

05-100-P01ever since the War, of Col. N. D. 05-102 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 05 - Folder 2, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 102

05-102-P01whisky for neuralgic rheumatism, and like every body else who uses whisky of any temperament, I sometimes used too much, but I never did use it regularly – and now for a great while I have abandoned the prescription, and I do not use any intoxicating drinks, not even berry wine, or cider, as the whole citizens of this Town will testify, and if any man says that I ever neglected my professional engagements on account of whisky he tells that which is absolutely untrue, I do use two cups of Coffee per day, I smoke tobacco and sometimes a little Mullein prescribed by a Methodist Preacher several years ago, but my respect for ladies is too great to allow me to chew tobacco, and this is the extent of my stimulants, Isaac N. Wright, Col. Brashears & Mr Dill & others who reside here are my clients – they have known me from infancy & ought to know what is best, One other party said that she would have nothing to do with it – her and her husband both told me the same, and the latter lately – the other resident party employed a Counsel who came and wanted to join me in the bills, but said he also represented a lot of second Cousins, I told him that his clients interests conflicted, that my bills denied second Cousins having any interests, and that I should comply with the rules of the Bar, that is, when parties originate a suit, and some in interest don’t want to employ their Counsel in a partition, they are made defendants & I would do so in this case, as I did not want to practice Law for persons who did not desire me to – he said he had not investigated the rights of the parties – I replied “that I had” And now I do not desire to take a Lawyer on the cases with me and teach him the law and pay him for the privilege by a division of my fees. Let me hear from you immediately and if you desire further inquiries I will cheerfully answer you – Very Respectfully B. F. Lillard
05-102-P02[[margin]] caused from Army wounds [[/margin]]

05-103 · manuscript

05-103-P01P. S. In addition to what I have said I will state that I have been informed by one of your true friends, that you have been written to, to turn your back on me and to employ a certain other Lawyer here – I will now proceed to give you the origin of that – Mr Alexander sent for the old Gentleman to meet him in Murfreesboro one day this week – he did so – they went to the Office of Mr Alexander’s Lawyer, and the three held a long [[deleted: Counsel]] [[inserted: Consultation]], the result of which was, that he went from that Law Office, and consulted with another Lawyer, and then he wrote his letter suggesting to you to employ that Lawyer – Against this Attorney I have not a word to say, nor will I be so uncharitable as to say aught against any brother Lawyer – but no Lawyer is infallible, and a Lawyer is as liable to be imposed [[inserted: on]] as any one else, when the case is stated to him from the opposite side, and that too by an old person whose prejudices can be worked upon – Why did not Mr. Alexander bring into the Consultation your Cousin Albert Jetton whose store is within 75-ft of that Law Office, It seems to me that you ought to be able to see through all this, My bills and work have won the battle unless trickery turns the tide, but I am determined to head off any such warfare, and if any party gives aid and comfort to the enemy to defeat their own rights they shall do so with their eyes open – If Captured by the enemy of this Lawsuit they shall be willing Captures – I further learned that Mr Alexander’s Lawyer said that he could not

I have set out to defeat these parties & I intend to do it, by every thing that an honorable man can do, nor do I intend to permit unjust and wrongful impressions to go out against me, I have as many diplomas from as good Universities as this Country affords, my Law Diploma was signed 25 years ago by Judges Green & Caruthers of Cumberland University, the ablest Jurists Tennessee ever had, and I defy any living man to point to a single stain of dishonor on my character – You yourself ought to be able from what I have informed you to understand all these matters – I would suggest that you lay all of my letters before some good Lawyer of your County, and I know what advice he would give you – There is nothing in this letter intended to reflect on your conduct towards me, Everything is written with the kindest of feelings – because I appreciate fully what has acted on your mind – Very Respectfully B. F. Lillard

05-108-P01any agreement between Alexander & wife as to the reinvestment of the proceeds I am not as yet advised – If this deed had been properly executed the sale would have converted the land into personalty and it would be absolutely the husband’s money,
05-108-P02My remark in my letter was simply the conclusion of one of my clients as to the purpose of the Conversion – One of them related to me that he was at Mr A’s house on one occasion when Mr A. argued the propriety of selling out and purchasing property in Town and appealed to him for approval and he did not seem to like his answer or rather seemed displeased at it, which was against Town property &c, As to fees and the costs we are not allowed as in your State to take Contingent fees, it would be champertous and a suit so taken by an Attorney would be dismissed, but of course Lawyers do not charge so high where there is no recovery – we look to amount involved &c, I can say this much that we will have no Controversy about the fees, and as Mr. Dill’s letter indicates we may avoid litigation, and simply have partition bills, and if it becomes necessary to litigate about the 640 Acre Tract of land I will notify you of all the particulars and await your advice before using the ladies names as Complainants – The parties are very much scattered, and I desire to commence proceedings in time for next Court and hope you will answer me immediately, Very Respectfully B. F. Lillard.

05-115 · manuscript

05-115-P01P. S. J. N. Spence the soninlaw of Mrs Bivins informed me that Mr A. M. Alexander about two weeks ago said that if they found no will that he did not want any lawsuit and that he would give up the lands, It is reasonable to suppose that a will conveying such a large estate and disposing of it would not be hid way in a corner – None has as yet been found and you may rest assured that none will be produced,
05-115-P02Now as to my opinion about the recovery of the 640 Acre Tract of land – I have fully investigated the question and I am fully satisfied that the deed can be declared null and void, but whether the parties will seek to do this is a subject for their own consideration, it is my duty as a Lawyer to point out the rights and it is the clients privilege to exercise it, Mr Alexander is rich outside of this – If he was needy and to take from him the benefits of this 640 Acre tract of land would leave him poor I think the moral question would enter and I am free to confess that I could not very zealously prosecute a suit to recover it – I do not think any such considerations apply – We do not know what induced her to join in the deed, but we do know that for years she had refused to sell it, we further are informed that she said that her Father on his death bed told her that he made no will, and asked her to promise the same and that she said that she had so promised and that she did not want Mr Alexander’s kin to have her property – So the law of the land was her will – If she had desired it she could have determined it otherwise, I do not even suppose that she knew her personal estate would go to her husband, and the taking of her Privy Examina

05-123-P01Cross Creek Travis Texas August the 30th 1869
05-123-P02George & Lizzie, your letters were duly and gladly rec, for I am ever anxious to hear from home, or Brushy and its vicinity — I've nothing very interesting to offer at present. The weather is extremely hot and dry in this section since the ((flood)) as I term it and in consequence a great deal of sickness below this Mostly Chills and fever
05-123-P03I was riding, three days last week trying to rent Me a place nearer Austin But failed All occupied for another Year. and the people not all But a great many sick for the season
05-123-P04I have become a little dissatisfyed with the Mountains Oweing to the roughness of the road and distance from Austin I was fully determined to move to Arkansas this fall. But I must Confess that the grief and tears of a good Old motherinlaw is a preventative, at least as long as She lives and possibly She may out live Me.
