06-013 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 13

06-013-P01Readyville March 20 1883 Miss Lizzie Douglas My Dearest Cousin Your very kind letter was recieved in due time and I was very glad to learn that you were all well and know that you have not forgoten me. Willie & myself are both as well as usual The Kin & friends are tolerble [[inserted: well]] at present. Mary Lees Babe little Granville has been very sick but is better to day Paps health has improved very much I think he looks [[inserted: now]] better than he has in Several months Adele spent Several days with me last week she is looking well and is very much pleased with her new home Bro Mcguffy has moved to his new home they have a very nice house & Yard and are

Editorial note Letter continues on scans 14–15. The year is positioned above the date.

06-014 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 14

06-014-P01much pleased with their Surroundings I am very glad to have Jennie So near me it is only a half a mile to her house and we visit each other often Aunt Sallie is still in west Tenn I dont [[inserted: know]] whether She will come back this Spring or not we [[inserted: have]] not heard from her in Some time I [[inserted: have]] been gardening Some but we have had So much Cold weather our vegatables are not doing much good we had quite a Snow Storm yest but the Snow is all gone this morning and it is very pleasant out of doors in the Sun I have Such a lonely time here only a little negro with me Willie is at home of nights but gone every day he is working land over at his Gran Paps this year and is either over there or off with his Waggon hauling for
06-014-P02Some one [[inserted: all the time]] he never misses a day if he Can help it So you See I am very much confined at home and consequently very lonely. You wished to know the particulars of Cousin Rebeccas Alexanders death I did not know She was Sick until the night before She died I was at the funeral and learned there that She exposed herself getting up very early of mornings to look at the Comet which She was very much interested about She took a very Severe Cold and died from the effect of it I think She was in her right mind all the time during her Sickness Sat up eat her breakfast and talked with her friends in the morning and died that evening a few a Clock, As to the Lawsuit I know very little about it only you know just what I hear [[inserted: hearsay]] other People Say

Editorial note Two facing pages, transcribed left then right. The account of Rebecca Alexander’s illness and death is the writer’s report, not a medical determination.

06-015 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 15

06-015-P01The latest and most reliable I have heard is from Charlie he and Pap were at Cousin Andys last Satterday they found him in very poor health and in a great deal of Trouble he told them Cousin Rebecca will was stolen out of her draw two weeks before her death he is willing to give up the land does not wish to have a law Suit but wants to buy the land of the heirs has already made arangements to get the interest of Several of the Wright family The Jacob Wright Estate is said to be worth about $[[26000?]] and my own opinion is the heirs ought to have it as Cousin Andy will be a rich man without it My best love to you all write soon Most affectionately your Cousin L A Jetton
06-015-P02[[margin]] Lizzie please dont let any one read this poor Scribled letter I am ashamed of it but have got so out of the habit of writing I cant write fit to be seen [[/margin]]

Editorial note Concludes the letter begun on scan 13. The postscript is inverted above the main text. The story about the will is expressly reported through Charlie and Pap in the letter; it is not independently established by this transcript.

06-017 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 17

06-017-P01Readyville Tenn March the 3 1878 Miss Lizzie Douglas My very dear Cousin Your kind and interesting letter came to hand in due time and I hasten to reply. This leaves the family in rather poor health, Pap & Mother are both Complaining and the Children all have the Whooping Cough. Willie has it very bad and besides that he has had a spell of billious fever is sitting up a little this morning for the first time in Several days. Adele is quite sick with the Cough She has it the second time. Cousin Nellie is getting better but Charles Children are very bad off. Well the wedding Came off at the appointed time we had nice Crowd a good supper and every thing passed off quietly. Adele Willie and myself all went to the reception At Cousin Miriams they had a splendid dinner and all seemed to enjoy it very much Jimmie & Mary are going to live with Cousin Miriam as She is almost alone having only one Child single and he is hardly ever at home Jimmie and Mary Came over yes evening they are both well they have [[inserted: gone]] to Church to day at newhope We had Some fun out of them this morning
06-017-P02[[margin]] you must excuse all errors as the Children have kept up such a noise and talking I could hardly write at all will try to do better next time Yours as ever L A J [[/margin]]

Editorial note Continues on scan 18. The postscript is inverted above the opening date.

06-018 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 18

06-018-P01about their kin they both call their motherinlaws Cousin they say the kin is so mixt up they dont [[inserted: know]] how to claim it So they will just call them all the same they did before they were married. Well Lizzie Jimmie was a boy sixteen years old when we came here and the first time I met him I told may beth I had picked him out for my Soninlaw (just in fun you know) She was very indignant about it vowed She wouldnt have him I laugh at her [[deleted: now]] [[inserted: now]] about [[deleted: her]] Changing [[inserted: her]] mind she says people often do that it is their privledge The kin and friends are generaly well so far as I know We have had a great deal of bad rainey weather this winter we hardly ever [[inserted: have]] more than [[one or?]] good days at a time The farmers are getting discouraged about not getting to plough any Wheat Crops looks very fine. Dear Cousin you have my deepest sympathy in your great afflictions I loved Cousin Sue on earth I hope to meet her in heaven where Sorrows and partings are unknown I [[inserted: will]] have to Close for the present Tell uncle Jimmy Mary is going to write to him before long and Send him hers & Jimmies pictures Give my best love to the kin and please accept a large share your self I want you to write to me as often as you can as I am always so glad to get a letter from you Adele says tell [[Cousin S he?]] will write to her before long when She gets well of the whooping Cough the Children all send much love to you most affectionately your cousin L A Jetton

Editorial note Concludes scan 17. The words before “good days” and the crowded message after “Adele says” are uncertain and marked in the text.

06-020 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 20

06-020-P01Readyville April. 8th 1874 Miss Lizzie Douglas. Dear Cousin as it has been raining all the morning I did not go to school. So I thought it would be a good opportunity to write to you. This leaves us all well. I believe [[inserted: all]] of the kin are enjoying tolerbly good health except aunt Nellie. She has been very sick for some time. the Doctor thinks she is improving a little now. 06-048-P01Bible through I am afraid he will not do it though I keep encouraging him Although we are in [[illegible]] and have nothing like methodist preaching and Methodist doctrines yet I think I have a greater influence over my brothers now than I ever did and I feel the weight of the responsibility of it Oh! may God assist me to set a right example I think Grand Ma [[tries?]] hopes to win us all over to the Methodist church she seldom speaks of our difference in belief when she does it is in the kindest manner it is now so dark I can hardly see how to write May be after supper I will tell you something of my evening adventures untill then I will stop writing

06-048-P03Crops are tolerably good here corn is turning out pretty well Cotton better than I expected Bud Davy had a chill today but he and Frank were both up late this evening Pa went to Montgomery [[deleted: illegible]] [[inserted: to day]] Tuesday and I was very much surprised at not getting a letter or two he expects to go to Lowndes tomorrow & turn over and I will send this by him to mail there I intended writing you another half sheet at odd times but I do not know when there will be any passing to that again Lizzie please correct all the mistakes you see in my letters and tell me of them and I will do likewise by yours if you wish As ever your true friend Lizzie Graves

06-050 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 50

06-050-P01Mosshead Ala. Wednesday night Aug. 29th 1867 Dear Lizzie Your very welcome letter of the 25th July reached me about two weeks ago I read it while in bed I have been quite sick was in bed nearly a [[illegible]] it [[illegible]] caused from billiousness being sick when I got your letter and very busy since I got well I hope you will excuse me for not answering it sooner I have only partially regained my strength I am greatly reduced in flesh and very much reduced in color My sickness has served to make me look like an Ala. girl the first morning that I was able to fill my seat at the table some of them said “Cousin Lizzie your sickness shows right smartly on you” I said “Oh! it is only helping to make an Ala. girl of me” it seemed like they were going to [[inserted: quiz]] me they all commenced “yes you never have any sickness in Texas” said I “we dont have as many pale faces there as you do here” I was sick two weeks last summer but it didn't take
06-050-P02[[separate block: left-hand page; concluding page of this letter]] it out I would have liked so much to have gone I dont know whether there is an organized church and regular preaching down there or not if there is I hope we will get to go yet I havnt met up with any one yet who is a member of the Christian Church Pa and I are alone it makes me feel right badly I do wish there were a church in this neighborhood or even if it were not in this neighborhood if it were near enough for us to attend once in a while that would be better than not to go at all we have very good Methodist preaching here as there are none but Methodists living here we dont have series of doctrinal sermons like they do at Liberty Lizzie do you ever hear anything from Mr. Goodman these days poor man I reckon he will move and move until he settle in his grave and leave his family helpless it is a pity that he cant be contented to stay in one place I feel sorry for him and family Where is Mr. Austin and what is he doing any thing? he is very much related to Mr. goodman Lizzie I have written a letter to Jane and Mary which I hope you will pardon me for sending in yours give it to any of or all the boys and they will get some person to read it for them you can read it if you want to before you give it to them I hardly knew how to begin or how to end please write me a long letter I have written two [[illegible]] this contains more to write with much love yours Lizzie

Editorial note Two facing pages. The opening is on the right and continues on scan 51, left. Scan 51, right, continues onto the left-hand concluding page transcribed here. The concluding request to pass along a letter is historical source text, not an action requested of the reader.

06-054-P01I have been quite well since then Brothers are now at work trying to earn their bread by the sweat of their brow work seems to agree with Frank [[Thaddie?]] & Charles they grow like all weeds there has been so much [[illegible]] they have had to work harder than they otherwise would have done It rains here nearly every monday and it is not confined to Monday alone it frequently rains nearly all the week I spent quite a pleasant day yesterday Mrs. W— her daughter and Miss E— spent the day with us they are [[deleted: Though]] very sociable nice people the only family in the neighborhood that have visited us at all There was a May party given by the ladies at Tusk[[illegible]] the first of this month (or a very [[illegible]]) to decorate the graves of the Alabama confederate dead A few days after the white ladies had enjoyed their [[deleted: illegible]] on the confederate dead The colored ladies of the city of Montgomery formed a procession marched down to the graveyard loaded with flowers to decorate the graves of the Yankee dead Montgomery is quite a fast place they had quite a time decorating the church last Christmas The church convention is now in [[illegible]] for the purpose of uniting the Methodist Episcopal and Methodist Protestant churches again I dont know how they will make it I hope they will all

06-056-P01Mosshead [[deleted: Thursday]] [[inserted: Friday]] night Nov. 20th 1868 My dear Lizzie I commence a letter to you tonight, not knowing when I will finish it. 06-057 · manuscript

06-057-P01ever saw. Sanfrancisco is far a head of Montgomery in the fashions if not ahead of New York a person has to be rich to move in the first circle there My cousins found it too gay for them, so they moved about 500 five hundred miles south of that to Los angeles, it is a very quiet and flourishing place. Uncle J. says he is very anxious to move his family out there, but is not able, but I think the Earth quakes they have been having out there have shaken his determination more than any thing else, he has bought a home in Greenville, about thirty miles from here, and they will all move from here in a week or two you know I will be lonesome I have had three room mates all the time, and to be left alone all at once I will feel it very sensibly, but I will have plenty to keep me busy and I expect the time will pass very rapidly Lizzie I was so glad to get your letter I had felt a good deal like scolding but you are so good to acknowledge your wrong and make such a good promise that I will not quarrel with
06-057-P02you this time especially as I have been so slow about answering it I must say though that I dont think your excuse for not writing was a very good one, viz, that I was enjoying my self so well you didn't think you could increase my enjoyment I never expect to enjoy my self so much that it would not be increased by a letter from home. Cousin Mary and I have had a good deal of pleasure, riding horse back since the working season has been over, that is generally our resort for pleasure We have been having a good many “beaus” since Cousin M. came Last Sunday was a week, we had four at once, and such a time as we did have one of them would say “yes” or “no” to every thing one said, and one would only agree with us in every thing we said, and one would try to tease us about every thing we said, and the other one seemed to want to say every thing he knew, (which seemed to be very little) I felt like I would rather they had stayed at home and let me spend the evening alone last saturday the one that loved to tease so well [[deleted: illegible]] and really the choicest of the four came

06-066 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 66

06-066-P01have any doubt but what they will take ours. It is now currently reported and believed, that the wheat which the Farmers carried in before the tythe tax, was due, in order that the Soldiers might get some flour has been sold by speculators. They say they would manage it this way; they would [[store?]] it [[illegible]] where a board would be appointed to examine it. They would condemn it as inferior flour, and it would be sold, they would then sell it at an enormous price and pocket the proceeds. There was a man here the other day from Nacochitoches who says this is so, and that Magruder is at the head of it. One thing I know, that a great deal of wheat has been ground up and taken off; and I reckon you could testify, that the Soldiers dont get much of it. What if old Magruder orders you away from Sabine about the time Joda gets back, and takes all of your flour and bacon away from you? We are going to send you a sack of flour a piece and a good supply of fat bacon which I hope will serve to make your fish a little more savory.
06-066-P02[[illegible]] do live on such poor diet and then to think it is all on account of the Speculators!
06-066-P03[[margin]] [[illegible]] and [[illegible]] all [[/margin]]

06-069 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 69

06-069-P01Brushy April 21st 1865 Dear George:
06-069-P02We were somewhat surprised last tuesday evening — the 18th by the arrival of Joda; we were all truly glad to see him you may be sure. I was a little disappointed in not getting a letter from you, but suppose you have written by mail since your return from Velasco. I am anxious to hear something about Dock, and the something you would tell of next time which you spoke of in yours of the 5th inst. has accomplished its purpose — excited my curiosity. The thread you sent by J. M. was very acceptable, it will save me many a turn of my “Piano.” The pins — although I had plenty you left before — do not come amiss. I have a little speculation in my head, which, if I was sure it would turn out right, I would write you several little articles to buy that you might keep your eyes open for such bargains: but, as I am not sure of accomplishing it, I will just tell you my plan. It is to send down some flour by Joda that Laura and I have. There is only six hundred pounds of it, and Joda says if he gets Transportation at all, he will take it. But he does not know yet whether he will get any thing to transport, untill he hears from Mr Bell. If he takes a waggon we will try to send you a little something to sweeten your tooth. I would like for you to look around a little, and see if a side saddle could be got in Houston. Laura is very anxious to get one, and if the flour speculation succeeds she might stand a chance to get one, as Joda says waggons are continually passing from Houston to Dallas

06-070 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 70

06-070-P01I recd a letter from Laura this evening. she seems to be very well satisfied in her new situation. says she has from 16 to 19 schollars, with a prospect for more. I suppose it is better than to be carding and spinning but take me from friends and Home, and place me among strangers, and I would feel that I was left to pine neglected, and to “perish in forgetfulness” — to be poetical. Cousin Mollie has just returned from school and is going to stay all night with me so I must quit.
06-070-P02Saturday evening 22nd Today has been bright and clear, quite an uncommon thing: the wind has been from the north since day before Yesterday, and last night we had frost enough to kill most of our beans, though I do not think the fruit is injured. Cousin M. amused me last night by relating some of her school exploits, and altogether the time was passed off quite pleasantly. She is going to Liberty; A mrs. Bishop is teaching there now. She is from Dallas, and some rather quiter, I mean — could teach almost every thing the Dead Languages included. But May says it is with difficulty she can find the answers in old Kirkham, so I suppose the Living have become Dead to her. Some foul play has been carried on, up here of late. Several horses have been stolen, and two Stiles boys who lived over in the neck and were deserters, were suspected. A company of men got after them, and caught one of them — the Youngest; he acknowledged to stealing and said his brother was concerned in it. They hung him on the same limb Stanley was hung on — so we have heard. They tell various tales about the other. one is that they shot him and broke his arm but did not take him. Another, that they took him, and hated to shoot him while a prisoner, so they guarded him loosely so he would try to make his escape, which he did. and then they shot him and broke his arm but he made his escape

Editorial note The account of violence explicitly includes hearsay (“so we have heard”) and alternative stories. These are the writer’s reports; no independent verification is implied. Text continues on scan page 71.

06-071 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 71

06-071-P01Last Sunday morning a party of 8 or 10 men passed Mrs Campbells — aunt [[Bats?]] house — with one of the Stileses. Jim Floyd was one of the party. a young man who was at Mrs Campbells asked Floyd what they were going to do with him. He replied, they were going to take the left hand road with him. The same day Jennie Bell saw a party enter the big thicket at their wood yard. They either took him in there and hung him, or went in to hunt him, after he made his escape. One thing is very certain to my mind, that is if they ever captured him, he never made his escape. Perhaps they merited death, but I dread the consequences of this mob law. We know not, but this is the beginning of terrible times. I hate the thought that Brushy [[deleted: should]] should be the place of the beginning of such things. I would feel a great deal better if I knew that man was not hanging over in that thicket.
06-071-P02Fayette has not yet returned; the last news from them, Bob was having the chills, and was trying to get a furlough to come with F.

Editorial note Continuation of the letter begun on scan page 69. The writer distinguishes belief from knowledge in her account of the suspected hanging.

06-072 · manuscript

06-072-P01Henry and Ellen went up last wednesday, so we are once more by our selves. Henry helped Father to sow his grass seed before he went away. 06-072-P02Tomorrow, brother Methodist Jones, will hold forth at Liberty. I will try to attend as I have not heard a discourse since the 1st of March.
06-072-P03Joda says tell you he is at home safe, and a plowing. We will try to keep him busy while he is at home. There are so many of us here now that we are almost obliged to have some more room. We recd two letters from [[Fran?]], last dated 26th of March. He is very anxious to come home, but thinks it uncertain about getting to come. There is one thing I would like very much to have, a pair of Scissors if you can get them cheap, but do not inconvenience yourself about them.
06-072-P04With best love to Warren and yourself I remain Lizzie

06-087 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 87

06-087-P01[[Wednesday?]] night 15th This beautiful day has been passed very busily and pleasantly by me, we have been putting in a piece of cloth, and this evening [[Colburn?]] and Henry R. came over. [[Colburn?]] came to stay all night to go a turkey hunting with Joda in the morning, but was disappointed as Joda went to Dallas to day to see Watts having failed to see him at Ingeville as he went to Johnson; but they have let their plans to have a good hunt while Joda stays. You ought to be here to hear the turkeys gobble of a morning and see some of our beautiful pet deer. They are very gentle, since you have ceased to torment them. We also have another pet up in these western thickets, which [[illegible]] we suppose to be a panther. There are a kind of pets I would rather would be a little more distant. On the subject of John Turneys, the people of [[Tennessee?]] are anxious that he shall get a six mule team if he can, if not two four mule teams; They seem anxious to send plenty up there to the Co.
06-087-P02Yours of March 30th we recd last Sunday. You say dont get too fast on the peace questions I will not, for I never was as sanguine as Father and [[Louisa?]] we all told them they were too fast. But I always have and always will think there is something wrong about this cotton trade. You have seen before this the account of the threatened invasion of Tex. from Ark. of twelve thousand men with a train of four hundred waggons. I rather reckon you have not seen the accounts of the wise disposal Genl McCullough has made of his men of late; or such items as these seldom go through the Telegraph. He appointed a day for them all to rendezvous at a certain place. Some of them being at home on furlough two hundred of them had arrived when they were surrounded by a Regmt and taken prisoners and then sent off to their respective commands where they had originally belonged, or to their enrolling officers. just turned out as I always expected it. un-fortunately a great many had not got there when the two hundred were captured. [[deleted: among them William Duff and his]] nephews Jno Smith who have returned to this section and report the [[illegible]]. They have joined Co. with [[illegible]]. This movement on the part of McCullough has rendered these men desperate. Rome has now gone to Wax. with a waggon master who he is acquainted with and who he thinks is his best friend, to try to get to drive a team from San Antonio to Eagle Pass. they say the Govt wants men who will bring their own teams. It will suit with R. if he can get into it but I cant help but think it is a trick to get him into the Service, though it may turn out that my fears are all groundless.

Editorial note The weekday is tentative; it has not been changed to agree with the preceding page’s Thursday heading. Several personal names remain unresolved. The writer reports McCullough’s actions without independent verification.

06-089 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 89

06-089-P01Brushy Nov. 13th 1864 Dear George
06-089-P02You will have to forgive my delay, my excuse is busy times. This morning Lou and I went to meeting to Liberty to hear Mrs Justins funeral preached; it was to have been preached at the Colony last Sunday, but Mr Haynes had been sick and was not able to preach. To day the house was cramed jamed and running over. A goodmany of the Colony were up and a greatmany from about Shilo. Five preachers: Haynes, Justin, Maley, White, and Berry. Mr Haynes gave us a long tedious discourse from the last clause of the 4th vs. of the 11th ch. Hebrews. He spoke fully two hours, and a good portion of his sermon was about the depreciation of the currency (I am not talking about the “old” and “new issue”) but the money itself. The “old issue” was fully represented to day; and judging from the display have no idea of being repudiated soon. Em. and Liz. were there I just got to speak to them, had no time to talk. I wish you had been there to see all of the people. Times are about as they were when you were here; a good deal of work to do and not much time to visit. We assisted aunt Bettie in getting ready to move, she did not get her thread ready for me to weave, but got some jeans from Ellen, and we made the little boys coats. She got off on the morning of the 10th

06-090 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 90

06-090-P01She took Betsy and chickens, left the spinnack and what few cows she had. I think [[her will intend?]] coming back to attend to them. Father bought a sow and five shoats from her for ten dollars specie, so I reckon we are safe in the meat line for another year. — that is if we can get corn raised to feed them on. Father has reserved twenty acres for [[corn?]] and hungarian. Laura wrote to you, Gibson was to fix the fence; she was mistaken that was not in the bargain; he was just to raise the wheat, cut it and thrash it and put a fourth in the granary. How the old fence is ever to be fixed up is more than I can tell but I hope it will all come out right. The well holds out finely about a dozen mules and horses are watered out of it twice and three times a day. It gets down pretty low, but hardly gets muddy, and by the next time is as full as ever. If I had any hopes of the war ending soon I would feel so much better but from the tone of the papers, we need not look for peace in four more years. Old Abe is elected in all probability and [[Childsy?]] thinks it is best for if Mc. Clelland is elected says he, propositions will be made which some of the States will accept, and then we are ruined. So it seems he is for four more years warfare.
06-090-P02The wind was blowing cold from the north on last monday evening we were all sitting round the fire, when we heard [[coo a night owl?]] we looked out and sure enough there she was. [[Childsy?]] was nearly frozen, but a warm fire soon set him to talking, and he and Mr Grounds (a soldier that came with him) kept us laughing nearly all the time they were here.

Editorial note The wording before “coming back” and the night-owl phrase remain tentative after review. The correspondent’s name appears to read Childsy; comparison with the physical original would help resolve its spelling.

Bates’ Reg which is stationed at Velasco, he says he expects they will remain there this winter. He winds up his letter by saying “I am a peace man, let it come as it may.” Ed Dickson is now at Tyler guarding prisoners. He deserves some credit for taking the trouble to send the socks over to Dock. Many a soldier, these scarce times would have kept them. [[Rome?]] it seems has a notion of hanging on to the service. The last letter Sister got from him he told her to buy two more waggons (a horse and an ox waggon) and come to him in the spring. Sister is mightily pleased, she has been wanting to leave here a long time. She thinks she can rent a house in San Antonio and send the children to school

06-092 · manuscript

06-092-P01Wednesday [[eve?]] 16th When I commenced writing I expected to send this to the office before now, but it seems like a hard matter to get a letter to the office these days. Warren not got down; he can get no conveyance to bring Beck down while he stays; he wrote he would be here on the 10th of Dec. on his way back to Sabine. I suppose he got his furlough lengthened. On last monday Mr. Marshalls family [[deleted: was]] rolled in from Ark. composed of two sons and six daughters. They are greatly dissatisfied down on the old man for marrying, and wish they were back in Ark. they say they had plenty there of every thing and a good house to live in. The jayHawkers were the only ones that pestered them much. They say the feds treated them better than our folks. The old man has rented Lous house for them, he is going to build a chimney in it before they move there.
06-092-P02As an other item of news I will tell you about [[Lidda?]] Bakers nose. Dr. Mc. [[Curdy?]] is making her a new one; he made a gold nose and then cut a piece of skin out of her forehead and twisted it around and covered the gold. when it knits at the sides he is going to cut it loose at the top and straighten it out: He says he is going to make her a lip when her nose gets well and then take her for pay. My paper is full and I will have to quit I reckon you are wishing I had. I cant send this to the office before Sunday. Affectionately Lizzie
06-092-P03[[margin]] Jane Green died last Saturday night with pneumonia. [[/margin]]
06-092-P04[[margin]] Aunt Bettie told me to tell you to write to her [[illegible]] Ark. [[/margin]]

06-094 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 94

06-094-P01Brushy Texas No. 4 Feb. 19th 1864. Brother George:
06-094-P02Yours of the 7th and 8th came to hand this evening, and although short, gave us great satisfaction. When the Sundays mail fails to bring us any letters, as it did last Sunday, we are always uneasy until we do hear from you. Brushy has been in the receipt of various news to day; Laura got a letter from Fayette, M Orr one from Fayette, Mary and I one apiece from Jim.
06-094-P03They have come safely through all the fights except the slight wound Jim rec. at Chickamauga. They both say they are tired of it. Jim says “he can stand the fatigue and hardships all very well, till it comes to the fighting fighting, and then when he sees his comrades falling around him, it is all he can do to persuade himself not to run.” Tole Hamilton fell at Ringold, in the retreat. he was shot through the breast, and only lived a few minutes. Poor fellow he has gone like many another from home and kindred, never more to return. He leaves a widow and child. The boys write home that two Goodloes (Aquilla’s sons) have deserted. And whose name do you expect to hear [[illegible]]? Tell no other than Squire. but uncle [[Givans?]] says this must [[illegible]] out of the family I know not why unless his boys wrote it home is the reason. But Squire is safe over the Border, where a many another poor Dixie boy wishes he was I expect. But Squire is not the only one of your acquaintance. The boys say that Charly Brown has written to them, that Will is safe with his kindred in a Northern Clime, hearty and well. Now dont you reckon that Will G. and Will Brown are having a happy time? Mary G. is perfectly satisfied about Billy. From the tone of Jodas letter, he seems to think your Batt. is disgraced worse than any other body of men in the Army, but he is behind the times, and had as well quit his noise. Tom [[Warthen?]] writes home that they have to keep a guard round Gurleys reg. to keep them from deserting. We hear there all of Murrays Texas Co. have left but Sixteen. I just give that at what it is worth. I am mistaken about Will G. being with

06-097 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 97

06-097-P01No. 13. Brushy, June 28th 1864. Dear Brother,
06-097-P02I have sorrowful news to tell you this morning. Uncle Alfred is dead. Mr Orr recd a letter from Henry yesterday, confirming what we had heard before. He died late Sunday evening the 5th of this month, just 18 days after he was wounded. Poor Aunt Bettie is allmost crushed beneath the blow. As soon as she heard it was really true, she fainted away, and they thought for a while she would never revive again. Ever since, she has had cramping spells, and sometimes loses her breath. I have just returned from there, Mary Orr and I staid with her last night. I fear she will have a severe spell of sickness first — if she ever does get over it.
06-097-P03He writes that the Drs made a good examination of his wound just a few days before he died, (which they had not been permitted to do previously, on account of the swelling) and discovered that the bone had been considerably fractured, just below the knee cap, and upon pressing it a good deal of matter run out with the oil of the knee cap.

06-098-P01[[separate block: left-hand page]] So you see it was a good deal worse, than was at first thought. Bob [[Head?]] and his lady called upon us yesterday morning; he is rapidly recovering from his wound and will start to his command next week. He says Uncle A. was a good soldier — he watched him during the fight and he appeared to be the coolest man in the Co. Harper got in the other day, he says Parsons men have been ordered to Houston. By the by Who is Coz G’s correspondent.
06-098-P02Well the Methodists are having another grand time at Liberty; their meeting commenced last Thursday night, and will probably end to night (tuesday). I have not attended except Sunday, there was so many people there that day, they had to move the seats out under the trees. I did not know half the people that were there. Parson Sansom preached in the morning, [[Coltr V.?]] in the evening. Had a splendid dinner on the ground and pretty girls were plenty, but thats no news to you.

He says Jim is as lazy as he can be, just lolls around camp, and he accuses him sometimes of being in love, but cant tell how it is as he never gets any letters and he is sure he never sees any of the girls, unless it is in his sleep.

06-103 · manuscript

06-103-P01Ann & Cynthia have just passed by going from aunt Catherines and say Warren Payne is dying he has been sick for some time. Lizzie and I will go up to sit up to night.
06-103-P02Warren Payne is the youngest child of Preston Payne whom aunt [[inserted: Catherine]] took when he was only nine days old and has kept him ever since. Had he have lived seven more days he would be seventeen months old. His disease was chronic diarhea. Lynn Norman is at home on furlough; he has been out on the RioGrande for some months but is on his way to La. He has to report at Sabinetown on the eighth of Sept. He says the Reg. to which Eppa belongs has been ordered to Arizona. Another wildgoose chase like that of Sibleys Brigade. Gran Jetter is still at home. I dont think he is in much of a hurry to return to his command as he has been digging a well for two or three weeks past and intends to finish dig and [[wole?]] it with white rock before he thinks about fixing to go back. His furlough has been out a long time and he says when he is in the army all he thinks about is how to manage to get another. His patriotism has cooled down several degrees below zero. Mr. Chappell and his son Bob were in fifty miles of their home the last that has been heard of them. They are now violent secesh, but doubtless have got home and taken the oath to the feds long ago to keep out of the service in Dixie. Miss Ada Chappell is living at Gran Jettons and she is so patriotic that she says she had rather her father and brothers would all be killed than for this confederacy to go down. but she knows they are all out of danger now for Dock deserted and went home from La. last fall you can now form some idea of what kind of folks they are. Sue
06-103-P03[[margin]] I wont write any more while Lizzie is at home I am not well [[illegible]] [[/margin]]

06-109-P01No. 16. Brushy Texas, Sept. 6th 1864 Dear Brother
06-109-P02I will again attempt to write. Last thursday I went up to the Office, and got yours of 10th ult. also the knife you sent by Mr. Collins, he passed a little while before I got to the office. I was sorry I did not get to see him. On yesterday I recd yours of 21st August; Lou, Jodas of the 25 which is the latest from you. I also received one from Frances Blair, who by the way is as good at letter writing, as conversation, and if you have ever been in her company much you know she is interesting. They were all well. Thomas has not yet gone into the service.
06-109-P03Jeppe arrived this evening. He brings no news, left all well on Hack Creek though there is a good deal of sickness in the neighbourhood. Henry has had a gooddeal of practice of late; he has lost two patients, one of them was Daniel H. Willbanks, Warren [[deleted: knows]] [[inserted: knew]] him. Sallie Willbanks married Dr. Orr, and they have parted.
06-109-P04Jeppe gathered his corn before he came down, made a hundred and thirty bushels. There has been several very sudden deaths in this section of late. Nancy Hancock, (Stout that was) was taken sick on Sunday and died on the next wednesday. They say after she died her eyes poped out of her head. While they were going to bury her, her little brother was taken suddenly, and died [[deleted: illegible]] in two hours he had had a chill or two before, but got up and helped to catch a chicken that morning and ate a hearty breakfast. It is a strange disease. They did not complain of any pain.
06-109-P05[[margin]] Father has sold old Toby to Mr. Martin, for twenty three dollars in specie. [[/margin]]

06-110 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 110

06-110-P01Mr. Telly — Nancy [[Culphers?]] husband — has been in bad health for a long time: on last Sunday morning a small red spot made its appearance on his arm, it spread rapidly and his arm swelled all over, mortification took place and he died on monday. I have heard of other sudden deaths, it is all thought to be the same disease, in different forms. J. Bell is now suffering severely from his wound. His shoulder is greatly swelled, and there is a place six inches long, in which he says he can feel the ball working back and forth. They think the ball will have to be cut out, but the Dr. thinks he had better not do it till the weather gets cooler.
06-110-P02Wednesday evening, 7th At 10 oclock this morning, Mr. L. Evans called on us, and left the books you sent, also the letter from Joda. You better reckon we were glad to see some one from the boys. He came riding up through the rain, which had been falling nearly all morning; he was in such a hurry he did not stay more than fifteen minutes, we wanted him to stay for dinner but he was in such a hurry he did not want to [[deleted: illegible]] talk about it. I reckon thats the way they all are when they get pretty close home. He will get home to night, says he left Sabine on the 31st thats only seven days on the road. Dont you call that pretty good traveling.
06-110-P03Mr Evans says your time will not come before Feb. he also says you could get leave of absence the same as he did. Now you know more about the matter than I do; but had you [[inserted: not]] better not come now, than wait till Feb, when perhaps the furlough business will all be stopped, or you sent off to La. or some where else? Thirty days is a short time for a man to come so far, he would not have long to stay at home but a short time is better than none. I am glad old Magruder is gone.
06-110-P04[[margin]] The little baby is five months old and weighs nine pounds. We thank you for the book [[inserted: you sent me]] [[illegible]] [[/margin]]
06-110-P05[[margin]] [[illegible]] says tell uncle [[illegible]] to see him [[/margin]]

06-111 · manuscript

ACU CRS MS5 - 06 - Folder 3, Correspondence and Legal Papers.pdf · Scan page 111

06-111-P01I will now have to tell you something more about the meetings which seem to be the most engrossing events of the day up this way. Every one preaches his own doctrine with all his might, consequently discussion [[inserted: is]] [[deleted: illegible]] rife. You wondered in one of yours, whether the good feeling which seemed to exist between different parties would continue to exist. I will give you a few items and you can draw your own conclusions: At our meeting in August it was announced that at the next appointment the meeting would be protracted, several eminent preachers were expected, and of course good preaching anticipated. Now this was not exactly in accordance with the feelings of our Methodist friends, so after studying on it awhile they appointed a meeting in Mr. [[Batakers?]] neighborhood embracing the same time! Not exactly as warm towards us as of yore, think you? The saying of Parson Hill, “now if me and my neighbor dont agree I just let him alone, dont go about him,” had sunk deep in their hearts, so union meetings were forgotten. Our meeting commenced at the appointed time, thursday night before the 1st Sunday. the congregations were small untill Sunday when, notwithstanding their efforts at “hedging” there were so many there, they had to move the seats out and preach under the grove. It is only the methodists that are so hostile. the presbyterians are not so afraid of the truth.
06-111-P02Bro Jones was [[inserted: the]] only preacher from a distance, he did most of the preaching. By request Parson Austin addressed the people Sunday evening, [[inserted: he preached]] from the words “It is finished”. He made a very good talk. There were no conversions, but four united with the church — Milton Rawlins, his wife, Sarah Selby, and Lizzie [[Burnard?]]. The meeting closed on monday night at which time Bro Jones preached one of the best sermons I ever heard on the unity of the church. The house was full and I hope a good

Editorial note Sectarian opinions are the writer’s own. Uncertain personal names remain marked in the text.

06-112-P01impression made. Parson Austin was not a regular attendant but heard several good sermons. It would be a treat indeed. I have only tasted one this year. But I would gladly take supper with you some night, if it consisted of bread and water only. I saw Walk Hatter the other day, he looks about as he used to. He was a prisoner four months, and says he fared well while in N’s hands. He says the Soldiers he talked with sayed the would fight us ten years but what they would whip us; that old Abe gave them plenty to eat and wear, and that is all they care for. You see I can write you long letters now; I have
06-112-P03[[margin]] done so this time, but I fear it will not be interesting to you. Lizzie [[/margin]]
